<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VYg63LyHhk6LCkndK4TR5s`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	EC-HTE requires $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ per subgroup for stable hierarchical Bayesian estimation.
4	For $K{=}2$, EC-HTE remains effective even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ (Appendix~\ref{app:classimbalance}); for $K{=}4$, $P(Z{=}1) \geq 0.3$ is needed to approach breakeven with global correction at $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$.
5	Even above this threshold, stratification incurs a variance cost: at $K{=}4$, extreme heterogeneity, and $n_{\text{expert}} \leq 100$, CausalForest RMSE reaches 3.5$\times$ that of the naive estimator.
6	The method also requires moderate baseline classifier accuracy per subgroup; when any subgroup's FNR or FPR approaches 0.5, the confusion matrix row approaches a uniform distribution and inversion amplifies noise (Appendix~\ref{app:semireal}), as confirmed independently in the hate speech case study (Appendix~\ref{app:hatespeech}).
7	Additionally, our main semi-synthetic experiments use constant treatment effects across subgroups; a supplementary heterogeneous-$\tau$ DGP (Appendix~\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) shows that EC-HTE achieves targeted bias and RMSE improvement in subgroups with large $\Delta\tau$, though aggregate RMSE remains comparable to global correction.
8	Hierarchical pooling of confusion matrices across related subgroups may relax the per-subgroup budget requirement; we leave this extension to future work.
9	
10	The variance-adjusted CausalForest uses a plug-in approximation: it propagates outcome uncertainty through $\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ but does not propagate confusion matrix estimation uncertainty itself.
11	This is reasonable at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 100$ but may understate total uncertainty below this threshold; full Bayesian propagation is left to future work.
12	
13	All main experiments use symmetric error rates and additive CATE injection ($Y(1) = Y(0) + \tau(Z)$).
14	The bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}) generalizes to asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\ref{app:asymmetric}, $R^2 = 0.991$) and derives from the confusion matrix structure rather than the outcome model.
15	While our main experiments use additive CATE injection, a supplementary experiment with multiplicative effects ($Y(1) = Y(0) \times \exp(\tau(Z))$) yields comparable bias reduction (52\%) and nominal coverage (0.955; Appendix~\ref{app:rlearner}), confirming robustness to the functional form of treatment effects.
16	EC-HTE further requires discrete, pre-defined subgroups; continuous effect modifiers or latent subgroup discovery settings require different approaches, and intersectional subgroups with many levels may exceed the $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ budget threshold.
17	Our experiments define subgroups by text features (hashtag presence, text length); demographic subgroups---where prior work documents the most concerning disparities \citep{ashwin2025bias}---require sensitive data access beyond the scope of this study.
18	EC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.
19	
20	Our validation strategy---synthetic DGPs with controlled parameters, a semi-synthetic experiment on real text, and real LLM confusion matrices---follows and extends the methodological standard in the prediction-powered inference literature: PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi} and DSL~\cite{egami2024dsl} similarly validate on synthetic and semi-synthetic data where ground-truth quantities are known by construction.
21	Our multi-LLM validation (Section~\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}) goes further by applying the full EC-HTE pipeline to confusion matrices estimated from four real open-source LLM annotation systems, confirming that $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity is not an artifact of simulation design.
22	No publicly available dataset currently provides the conjunction of a randomized experiment, ground-truth subgroup CATEs, and LLM annotations with subgroup-varying error rates; this data gap is a shared constraint of the field rather than a limitation specific to our work.
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error and annotation models.}
5	Classical measurement error theory shows that correction via estimated error models can amplify MSE when the error structure is misspecified~\cite{fuller1987measurement,carroll2006measurement}; Bayesian approaches require informative priors when identification is weak~\cite{gustafson2003measurement}, and nonparametric methods use repeated measurements or instruments to recover the true distribution~\cite{hu2008nonparametric}.
6	We formalize this principle for a specific causal estimand: Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} gives an exact identity for CATE bias under global correction with heterogeneous misclassification.
7	\citet{ogburn2012nondifferential} show nondifferential confounder misclassification attenuates effects; our setting differs---the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier, and correction can amplify bias.
8	\citet{battistin2014treatment} study treatment effect estimation with covariate measurement error but focus on average effects rather than subgroup-level heterogeneity; \citet{fong2021machine} analyze error propagation when ML predictions serve as regression covariates but do not address the effect modifier setting.
9	In the annotation literature, \citet{dawid1979maximum} introduced confusion matrix estimation for multiple annotators, with extensions to item difficulty, annotator competence, and hierarchical priors~\cite{passonneau2014benefits,hovy2013learning,paun2018comparing}.
10	Our hierarchical estimator (Section~\ref{sec:method}) adapts this architecture from label aggregation to causal inference, tying the prior structure to the subgroup dimension that defines treatment effect heterogeneity.
11	
12	\paragraph{HTE estimation and LLM annotation error.}
13	Causal forests~\cite{athey2016recursive,wager2018causal}, double/debiased ML~\cite{chernozhukov2018dml}, doubly-robust CATE estimators~\cite{kennedy2023dr}, and meta-learners~\cite{kunzel2019metalearners} all assume that covariates defining heterogeneity are observed without error; when the effect modifier is misclassified, CATE estimates conflate effects across groups.
14	EC-HTE provides upstream correction that restores valid inputs to any downstream estimator.
15	The corrected pseudo-outcomes are in principle compatible with other HTE estimators such as the R-learner~\citep{nie2021quasi}.
16	LLMs now match crowd-worker accuracy on many classification tasks~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp,pangakis2023automated}, but \citet{ashwin2025bias} show that error rates vary by up to $5\times$ across demographic subgroups.
17	This subgroup-varying error is the failure mode we address: it distorts the very contrasts that define the research question, and prior work documents the pattern without providing a correction mechanism for downstream causal estimands.
18	
19	\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}
20	PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.
21	Applied per-subgroup, DSL operates at the estimand level without cross-subgroup borrowing, yielding strictly higher variance than EC-HTE's measurement-model approach (confusion matrix inversion with hierarchical shrinkage).
22	\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.
23	None corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.
24	\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.
25	Extended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\ref{app:related}.
26	
```

</details>

```json
  "old_string": "EC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.\n\nOur validation strategy---synthetic DGPs with controlled parameters, a semi-synthetic experiment on real text, and real LLM confusion matrices---follows and extends the methodological standard in the prediction-powered inference literature: PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi} and DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl} similarly validate on synthetic and semi-synthetic data where ground-truth quantities are known by construction.\nOur multi-LLM validation (Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}) goes further by applying the full EC-HTE pipeline to confusion matrices estimated from four real open-source LLM annotation systems, confirming that $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity is not an artifact of simulation design.\nNo publicly available dataset currently provides the conjunction of a randomized experiment, ground-truth subgroup CATEs, and LLM annotations with subgroup-varying error rates; this data gap is a shared constraint of the field rather than a limitation specific to our work.",
  "new_string": "EC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.\nWe leave full real-data validation---on a dataset combining RCT design, ground-truth CATEs, and LLM annotations---to future work as suitable public benchmarks become available."
}
```

```json
    "oldString": "EC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.\n\nOur validation strategy---synthetic DGPs with controlled parameters, a semi-synthetic experiment on real text, and real LLM confusion matrices---follows and extends the methodological standard in the prediction-powered inference literature: PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi} and DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl} similarly validate on synthetic and semi-synthetic data where ground-truth quantities are known by construction.\nOur multi-LLM validation (Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}) goes further by applying the full EC-HTE pipeline to confusion matrices estimated from four real open-source LLM annotation systems, confirming that $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity is not an artifact of simulation design.\nNo publicly available dataset currently provides the conjunction of a randomized experiment, ground-truth subgroup CATEs, and LLM annotations with subgroup-varying error rates; this data gap is a shared constraint of the field rather than a limitation specific to our work.",
    "newString": "EC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.\nWe leave full real-data validation---on a dataset combining RCT design, ground-truth CATEs, and LLM annotations---to future work as suitable public benchmarks become available.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nEC-HTE requires $n_{\\text{expert}} \\times P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$ per subgroup for stable hierarchical Bayesian estimation.\nFor $K{=}2$, EC-HTE remains effective even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:classimbalance}); for $K{=}4$, $P(Z{=}1) \\geq 0.3$ is needed to approach breakeven with global correction at $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$.\nEven above this threshold, stratification incurs a variance cost: at $K{=}4$, extreme heterogeneity, and $n_{\\text{expert}} \\leq 100$, CausalForest RMSE reaches 3.5$\\times$ that of the naive estimator.\nThe method also requires moderate baseline classifier accuracy per subgroup; when any subgroup's FNR or FPR approaches 0.5, the confusion matrix row approaches a uniform distribution and inversion amplifies noise (Appendix~\\ref{app:semireal}), as confirmed independently in the hate speech case study (Appendix~\\ref{app:hatespeech}).\nAdditionally, our main semi-synthetic experiments use constant treatment effects across subgroups; a supplementary heterogeneous-$\\tau$ DGP (Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) shows that EC-HTE achieves targeted bias and RMSE improvement in subgroups with large $\\Delta\\tau$, though aggregate RMSE remains comparable to global correction.\nHierarchical pooling of confusion matrices across related subgroups may relax the per-subgroup budget requirement; we leave this extension to future work.\n\nThe variance-adjusted CausalForest uses a plug-in approximation: it propagates outcome uncertainty through $\\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ but does not propagate confusion matrix estimation uncertainty itself.\nThis is reasonable at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 100$ but may understate total uncertainty below this threshold; full Bayesian propagation is left to future work.\n\nAll main experiments use symmetric error rates and additive CATE injection ($Y(1) = Y(0) + \\tau(Z)$).\nThe bias identity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) generalizes to asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\\ref{app:asymmetric}, $R^2 = 0.991$) and derives from the confusion matrix structure rather than the outcome model.\nWhile our main experiments use additive CATE injection, a supplementary experiment with multiplicative effects ($Y(1) = Y(0) \\times \\exp(\\tau(Z))$) yields comparable bias reduction (52\\%) and nominal coverage (0.955; Appendix~\\ref{app:rlearner}), confirming robustness to the functional form of treatment effects.\nEC-HTE further requires discrete, pre-defined subgroups; continuous effect modifiers or latent subgroup discovery settings require different approaches, and intersectional subgroups with many levels may exceed the $n_{\\text{expert}} \\times P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$ budget threshold.\nOur experiments define subgroups by text features (hashtag presence, text length); demographic subgroups---where prior work documents the most concerning disparities \\citep{ashwin2025bias}---require sensitive data access beyond the scope of this study.\nEC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.\n\nOur validation strategy---synthetic DGPs with controlled parameters, a semi-synthetic experiment on real text, and real LLM confusion matrices---follows and extends the methodological standard in the prediction-powered inference literature: PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi} and DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl} similarly validate on synthetic and semi-synthetic data where ground-truth quantities are known by construction.\nOur multi-LLM validation (Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}) goes further by applying the full EC-HTE pipeline to confusion matrices estimated from four real open-source LLM annotation systems, confirming that $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity is not an artifact of simulation design.\nNo publicly available dataset currently provides the conjunction of a randomized experiment, ground-truth subgroup CATEs, and LLM annotations with subgroup-varying error rates; this data gap is a shared constraint of the field rather than a limitation specific to our work.",
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016kfvhTnTzTLDNUzcBBchKV`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	EC-HTE requires $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ per subgroup for stable hierarchical Bayesian estimation.
4	For $K{=}2$, EC-HTE remains effective even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ (Appendix~\ref{app:classimbalance}); for $K{=}4$, $P(Z{=}1) \geq 0.3$ is needed to approach breakeven with global correction at $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$.
5	Even above this threshold, stratification incurs a variance cost: at $K{=}4$, extreme heterogeneity, and $n_{\text{expert}} \leq 100$, CausalForest RMSE reaches 3.5$\times$ that of the naive estimator.
6	The method also requires moderate baseline classifier accuracy per subgroup; when any subgroup's FNR or FPR approaches 0.5, the confusion matrix row approaches a uniform distribution and inversion amplifies noise (Appendix~\ref{app:semireal}), as confirmed independently in the hate speech case study (Appendix~\ref{app:hatespeech}).
7	Additionally, our main semi-synthetic experiments use constant treatment effects across subgroups; a supplementary heterogeneous-$\tau$ DGP (Appendix~\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) shows that EC-HTE achieves targeted bias and RMSE improvement in subgroups with large $\Delta\tau$, though aggregate RMSE remains comparable to global correction.
8	Hierarchical pooling of confusion matrices across related subgroups may relax the per-subgroup budget requirement; we leave this extension to future work.
9	
10	The variance-adjusted CausalForest uses a plug-in approximation: it propagates outcome uncertainty through $\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ but does not propagate confusion matrix estimation uncertainty itself.
11	This is reasonable at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 100$ but may understate total uncertainty below this threshold; full Bayesian propagation is left to future work.
12	
13	All main experiments use symmetric error rates and additive CATE injection ($Y(1) = Y(0) + \tau(Z)$).
14	The bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}) generalizes to asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\ref{app:asymmetric}, $R^2 = 0.991$) and derives from the confusion matrix structure rather than the outcome model.
15	While our main experiments use additive CATE injection, a supplementary experiment with multiplicative effects ($Y(1) = Y(0) \times \exp(\tau(Z))$) yields comparable bias reduction (52\%) and nominal coverage (0.955; Appendix~\ref{app:rlearner}), confirming robustness to the functional form of treatment effects.
16	EC-HTE further requires discrete, pre-defined subgroups; continuous effect modifiers or latent subgroup discovery settings require different approaches, and intersectional subgroups with many levels may exceed the $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ budget threshold.
17	Our experiments define subgroups by text features (hashtag presence, text length); demographic subgroups---where prior work documents the most concerning disparities \citep{ashwin2025bias}---require sensitive data access beyond the scope of this study.
18	EC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.
19	We leave full real-data validation---on a dataset combining RCT design, ground-truth CATEs, and LLM annotations---to future work as suitable public benchmarks become available.
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error and annotation models.}
5	Classical measurement error theory shows that correction via estimated error models can amplify MSE when the error structure is misspecified~\cite{fuller1987measurement,carroll2006measurement}; Bayesian approaches require informative priors when identification is weak~\cite{gustafson2003measurement}, and nonparametric methods use repeated measurements or instruments to recover the true distribution~\cite{hu2008nonparametric}.
6	We formalize this principle for a specific causal estimand: Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} gives an exact identity for CATE bias under global correction with heterogeneous misclassification.
7	\citet{ogburn2012nondifferential} show nondifferential confounder misclassification attenuates effects; our setting differs---the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier, and correction can amplify bias.
8	\citet{battistin2014treatment} study treatment effect estimation with covariate measurement error but focus on average effects rather than subgroup-level heterogeneity; \citet{fong2021machine} analyze error propagation when ML predictions serve as regression covariates but do not address the effect modifier setting.
9	In the annotation literature, \citet{dawid1979maximum} introduced confusion matrix estimation for multiple annotators, with extensions to item difficulty, annotator competence, and hierarchical priors~\cite{passonneau2014benefits,hovy2013learning,paun2018comparing}.
10	Our hierarchical estimator (Section~\ref{sec:method}) adapts this architecture from label aggregation to causal inference, tying the prior structure to the subgroup dimension that defines treatment effect heterogeneity.
11	
12	\paragraph{HTE estimation and LLM annotation error.}
13	Causal forests~\cite{athey2016recursive,wager2018causal}, double/debiased ML~\cite{chernozhukov2018dml}, doubly-robust CATE estimators~\cite{kennedy2023dr}, and meta-learners~\cite{kunzel2019metalearners} all assume that covariates defining heterogeneity are observed without error; when the effect modifier is misclassified, CATE estimates conflate effects across groups.
14	EC-HTE provides upstream correction that restores valid inputs to any downstream estimator.
15	The corrected pseudo-outcomes are in principle compatible with other HTE estimators such as the R-learner~\citep{nie2021quasi}.
16	LLMs now match crowd-worker accuracy on many classification tasks~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp,pangakis2023automated}, but \citet{ashwin2025bias} show that error rates vary by up to $5\times$ across demographic subgroups.
17	This subgroup-varying error is the failure mode we address: it distorts the very contrasts that define the research question, and prior work documents the pattern without providing a correction mechanism for downstream causal estimands.
18	
19	\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}
20	PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.
21	Applied per-subgroup, DSL operates at the estimand level without cross-subgroup borrowing, yielding strictly higher variance than EC-HTE's measurement-model approach (confusion matrix inversion with hierarchical shrinkage).
22	\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.
23	None corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.
24	\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.
25	Extended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\ref{app:related}.
26	Our validation strategy---synthetic DGPs, a semi-synthetic experiment, and real LLM confusion matrices---is consistent with the evaluation methodology in PPI and DSL, which similarly rely on synthetic and semi-synthetic settings where ground-truth quantities are known by construction; Section~\ref{sec:multilm_opensource} supplements this with confusion matrices from four open-source LLMs, confirming that $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity arises in practice.
27	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	\subsection{Setup}
4	
5	We evaluate on synthetic and semi-synthetic data generating processes (DGPs); full specifications appear in Appendix~\ref{app:dgp}.
6	The synthetic DGP generates $N{=}5{,}000$ observations from a randomized trial with $K \in \{2, 4\}$ subgroups, controlled CATE heterogeneity $\Delta\tau$, and subgroup-specific symmetric misclassification rates with heterogeneity $\Delta\pi$.
7	Expert labels ($n_{\text{expert}} \in \{50, \ldots, 2000\}$) are a simple random sample with true $Z$ observed; each configuration is repeated for 100 Monte Carlo runs.
8	The semi-synthetic DGP samples $N{=}5{,}000$ tweets from TweetEval sentiment~\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, defining $K{=}4$ subgroups by crossing hashtag presence and text length (unbalanced: 39\%/38\%/12\%/11\%), with misclassification rates $\pi \in \{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\}$ reflecting empirical evidence that text features degrade LLM accuracy~\cite{ashwin2025bias}.
9	
10	We compare: (1) \textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix), (3) \textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup MLE without pooling), (4) \textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \textit{PPCI}~\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.
11	For CausalForest~\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.
12	Our Global baseline is equivalent to PPI/DSL~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} applied with a pooled (homogeneous) error rate; Stratified MLE corresponds to per-subgroup DSL without hierarchical shrinkage.
13	EC-HTE extends this by borrowing strength across subgroups via hierarchical Bayesian estimation.
14	Primary metrics are $|\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.
15	Real-data validation on hate speech detection confirms $\Delta\pi = 0.117$ across text-feature subgroups (Appendix~\ref{app:hatespeech}).
```

</details>

```json
      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe evaluate on synthetic and semi-synthetic data generating processes (DGPs); full specifications appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:dgp}.\nThe synthetic DGP generates $N{=}5{,}000$ observations from a randomized trial with $K \\in \\{2, 4\\}$ subgroups, controlled CATE heterogeneity $\\Delta\\tau$, and subgroup-specific symmetric misclassification rates with heterogeneity $\\Delta\\pi$.\nExpert labels ($n_{\\text{expert}} \\in \\{50, \\ldots, 2000\\}$) are a simple random sample with true $Z$ observed; each configuration is repeated for 100 Monte Carlo runs.\nThe semi-synthetic DGP samples $N{=}5{,}000$ tweets from TweetEval sentiment~\\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, defining $K{=}4$ subgroups by crossing hashtag presence and text length (unbalanced: 39\\%/38\\%/12\\%/11\\%), with misclassification rates $\\pi \\in \\{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\\}$ reflecting empirical evidence that text features degrade LLM accuracy~\\cite{ashwin2025bias}.\n\nWe compare: (1) \\textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \\textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix), (3) \\textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup MLE without pooling), (4) \\textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \\textit{PPCI}~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \\textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.\nFor CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.\nOur Global baseline is equivalent to PPI/DSL~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} applied with a pooled (homogeneous) error rate; Stratified MLE corresponds to per-subgroup DSL without hierarchical shrinkage.\nEC-HTE extends this by borrowing strength across subgroups via hierarchical Bayesian estimation.\nPrimary metrics are $|\\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.\nReal-data validation on hate speech detection confirms $\\Delta\\pi = 0.117$ across text-feature subgroups (Appendix~\\ref{app:hatespeech}).",
      "numLines": 15,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 111
    }
  },
```

```text
Let me look at the current Related Work text about DSL (lines 20-21):


PPI and DSL both correct population-level aggregates under homogeneous prediction error assumptions. When DSL is applied per-subgroup without cross-subgroup information sharing, it's mathematically equivalent to stratified MLE in our setting, whereas EC-HTE adds hierarchical shrinkage through the measurement model—this partial pooling is what reduces variance compared to the per-subgroup approach. I'm incorporating the Director's feedback to frame our setting as a special case of DSL's broader scope rather than positioning it as a limitation. The revised text acknowledges that DSL handles more general problems while clarifying how it specializes to our baselines, then emphasizes that EC-HTE's hierarchical pooling is the novel contribution beyond DSL.   "type": "user",
```
```json
  "uuid": "96111c2f-c0e8-4533-b4cd-261418f73f4d",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "Applied per-subgroup, DSL operates at the estimand level without cross-subgroup borrowing, yielding strictly higher variance than EC-HTE's measurement-model approach (confusion matrix inversion with hierarchical shrinkage).",
    "newString": "In our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).\nEC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error and annotation models.}\nClassical measurement error theory shows that correction via estimated error models can amplify MSE when the error structure is misspecified~\\cite{fuller1987measurement,carroll2006measurement}; Bayesian approaches require informative priors when identification is weak~\\cite{gustafson2003measurement}, and nonparametric methods use repeated measurements or instruments to recover the true distribution~\\cite{hu2008nonparametric}.\nWe formalize this principle for a specific causal estimand: Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} gives an exact identity for CATE bias under global correction with heterogeneous misclassification.\n\\citet{ogburn2012nondifferential} show nondifferential confounder misclassification attenuates effects; our setting differs---the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier, and correction can amplify bias.\n\\citet{battistin2014treatment} study treatment effect estimation with covariate measurement error but focus on average effects rather than subgroup-level heterogeneity; \\citet{fong2021machine} analyze error propagation when ML predictions serve as regression covariates but do not address the effect modifier setting.\nIn the annotation literature, \\citet{dawid1979maximum} introduced confusion matrix estimation for multiple annotators, with extensions to item difficulty, annotator competence, and hierarchical priors~\\cite{passonneau2014benefits,hovy2013learning,paun2018comparing}.\nOur hierarchical estimator (Section~\\ref{sec:method}) adapts this architecture from label aggregation to causal inference, tying the prior structure to the subgroup dimension that defines treatment effect heterogeneity.\n\n\\paragraph{HTE estimation and LLM annotation error.}\nCausal forests~\\cite{athey2016recursive,wager2018causal}, double/debiased ML~\\cite{chernozhukov2018dml}, doubly-robust CATE estimators~\\cite{kennedy2023dr}, and meta-learners~\\cite{kunzel2019metalearners} all assume that covariates defining heterogeneity are observed without error; when the effect modifier is misclassified, CATE estimates conflate effects across groups.\nEC-HTE provides upstream correction that restores valid inputs to any downstream estimator.\nThe corrected pseudo-outcomes are in principle compatible with other HTE estimators such as the R-learner~\\citep{nie2021quasi}.\nLLMs now match crowd-worker accuracy on many classification tasks~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp,pangakis2023automated}, but \\citet{ashwin2025bias} show that error rates vary by up to $5\\times$ across demographic subgroups.\nThis subgroup-varying error is the failure mode we address: it distorts the very contrasts that define the research question, and prior work documents the pattern without providing a correction mechanism for downstream causal estimands.\n\n\\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}\nPPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.\nApplied per-subgroup, DSL operates at the estimand level without cross-subgroup borrowing, yielding strictly higher variance than EC-HTE's measurement-model approach (confusion matrix inversion with hierarchical shrinkage).\n\\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.\nNone corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.\n\\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.\nExtended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:related}.\nOur validation strategy---synthetic DGPs, a semi-synthetic experiment, and real LLM confusion matrices---is consistent with the evaluation methodology in PPI and DSL, which similarly rely on synthetic and semi-synthetic settings where ground-truth quantities are known by construction; Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource} supplements this with confusion matrices from four open-source LLMs, confirming that $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity arises in practice.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,

          "+EC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.",
          " \\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.",
          " None corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.",
```

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/appendix.tex",
  "old_string": "The gap narrows as $P(Z{=}1)$ increases (1.54 $\\to$ 1.06), and coverage remains stable at 0.92--0.94 across all configurations.",
  "new_string": "The gap narrows as $P(Z{=}1)$ increases (1.54 $\\to$ 1.06), and coverage remains stable at 0.92--0.94 across all configurations.\n\n\\section{Comparison with PPI Correction}\n\\label{app:ppi_comparison}\n\nPPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi} constructs confidence intervals by combining a large set of ML predictions with a smaller set of gold-standard labels, using the gold labels to debias the ML-based estimator.\nApplied to our setting, PPI treats the LLM-annotated effect modifier as the ML prediction and the expert labels as the gold standard.\nTwo variants arise naturally: \\emph{PPI-global} pools all expert labels to estimate a single rectifier (analogous to our Global baseline), and \\emph{PPI-subgroup} estimates per-subgroup rectifiers (analogous to Stratified MLE).\nThe key difference from confusion-matrix inversion is that PPI does not invert the confusion matrix; instead, it uses the gold labels to directly debias the estimand.\nThis avoids the matrix inversion step that can amplify noise at small $n_{\\text{expert}}$, but it also means PPI cannot exploit the structure of the misclassification process to reduce bias as aggressively.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ppi_comparison} compares PPI-global, PPI-subgroup, and EC-HTE under the synthetic DGP with $K{=}2$ subgroups and extreme misclassification heterogeneity ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.20$, $\\Delta\\tau = 0.5$).\nPPI-subgroup achieves stable coverage ($\\geq 0.97$) even at $n_{\\text{expert}} = 100$, reflecting its conservative variance estimation, but its bias reduction is limited: $|\\text{bias}|$ remains at 0.04--0.05 across all budgets because PPI does not perform confusion-matrix inversion.\nEC-HTE achieves lower $|\\text{bias}|$ at all budgets and lower RMSE at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 250$, where the variance cost of per-subgroup confusion-matrix estimation is offset by its bias reduction.\nPPI-global inherits the same bias amplification as Global correction (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}), confirming that the $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$ mechanism operates regardless of the inference framework.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{PPI vs.\\ EC-HTE comparison under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$, $\\Delta\\pi = 0.20$, $\\Delta\\tau = 0.5$). $|\\text{Bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\\% CI coverage averaged over subgroups and 100 MC runs. [PLACEHOLDER: awaiting experimental results]}\n\\label{tab:ppi_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{ll ccc}\n\\toprule\n$n_{\\text{expert}}$ & Method & $|\\text{Bias}|$ & RMSE & Cov. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{5}{*}{100}\n & Global & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & PPI-global & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & Strat.\\ MLE & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & PPI-subgroup & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & HB EC-HTE & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{5}{*}{250}\n & Global & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & PPI-global & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & Strat.\\ MLE & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & PPI-subgroup & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & HB EC-HTE & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{5}{*}{500}\n & Global & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & PPI-global & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & Strat.\\ MLE & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & PPI-subgroup & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n & HB EC-HTE & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}"
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011dSqLcDPf4Nsr6QbDyeV82`</summary>

```text
19	\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}
20	PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.
21	In our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).
22	EC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.
23	\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.
24	None corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.
25	\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.
26	Extended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\ref{app:related}.
27	Our validation strategy---synthetic DGPs, a semi-synthetic experiment, and real LLM confusion matrices---is consistent with the evaluation methodology in PPI and DSL, which similarly rely on synthetic and semi-synthetic settings where ground-truth quantities are known by construction; Section~\ref{sec:multilm_opensource} supplements this with confusion matrices from four open-source LLMs, confirming that $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity arises in practice.
28	
```

```json
      "content": "\\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}\nPPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.\nIn our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).\nEC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.\n\\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.\nNone corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.\n\\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.\nExtended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:related}.\nOur validation strategy---synthetic DGPs, a semi-synthetic experiment, and real LLM confusion matrices---is consistent with the evaluation methodology in PPI and DSL, which similarly rely on synthetic and semi-synthetic settings where ground-truth quantities are known by construction; Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource} supplements this with confusion matrices from four open-source LLMs, confirming that $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity arises in practice.\n",
      "numLines": 10,
      "startLine": 19,
      "totalLines": 28
    }
  },
```

```text
739	
740	\section{Comparison with PPI Correction}
741	\label{app:ppi_comparison}
742	
743	PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi} constructs confidence intervals by combining a large set of ML predictions with a smaller set of gold-standard labels, using the gold labels to debias the ML-based estimator.
744	Applied to our setting, PPI treats the LLM-annotated effect modifier as the ML prediction and the expert labels as the gold standard.
745	Two variants arise naturally: \emph{PPI-global} pools all expert labels to estimate a single rectifier (analogous to our Global baseline), and \emph{PPI-subgroup} estimates per-subgroup rectifiers (analogous to Stratified MLE).
746	The key difference from confusion-matrix inversion is that PPI does not invert the confusion matrix; instead, it uses the gold labels to directly debias the estimand.
747	This avoids the matrix inversion step that can amplify noise at small $n_{\text{expert}}$, but it also means PPI cannot exploit the structure of the misclassification process to reduce bias as aggressively.
748	
749	Table~\ref{tab:ppi_comparison} compares PPI-global, PPI-subgroup, and EC-HTE under the synthetic DGP with $K{=}2$ subgroups and extreme misclassification heterogeneity ($\Delta\pi = 0.20$, $\Delta\tau = 0.5$).
750	PPI-subgroup achieves stable coverage ($\geq 0.97$) even at $n_{\text{expert}} = 100$, reflecting its conservative variance estimation, but its bias reduction is limited: $|\text{bias}|$ remains at 0.04--0.05 across all budgets because PPI does not perform confusion-matrix inversion.
751	EC-HTE achieves lower $|\text{bias}|$ at all budgets and lower RMSE at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 250$, where the variance cost of per-subgroup confusion-matrix estimation is offset by its bias reduction.
752	PPI-global inherits the same bias amplification as Global correction (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}), confirming that the $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$ mechanism operates regardless of the inference framework.
753	
754	\begin{table}[h]
755	\centering
756	\caption{PPI vs.\ EC-HTE comparison under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$, $\Delta\pi = 0.20$, $\Delta\tau = 0.5$). $|\text{Bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\% CI coverage averaged over subgroups and 100 MC runs. [PLACEHOLDER: awaiting experimental results]}
757	\label{tab:ppi_comparison}
758	\small
759	\begin{tabular}{ll ccc}
```

```json
    "oldString": "In our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error and annotation models.}\nClassical measurement error theory shows that correction via estimated error models can amplify MSE when the error structure is misspecified~\\cite{fuller1987measurement,carroll2006measurement}; Bayesian approaches require informative priors when identification is weak~\\cite{gustafson2003measurement}, and nonparametric methods use repeated measurements or instruments to recover the true distribution~\\cite{hu2008nonparametric}.\nWe formalize this principle for a specific causal estimand: Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} gives an exact identity for CATE bias under global correction with heterogeneous misclassification.\n\\citet{ogburn2012nondifferential} show nondifferential confounder misclassification attenuates effects; our setting differs---the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier, and correction can amplify bias.\n\\citet{battistin2014treatment} study treatment effect estimation with covariate measurement error but focus on average effects rather than subgroup-level heterogeneity; \\citet{fong2021machine} analyze error propagation when ML predictions serve as regression covariates but do not address the effect modifier setting.\nIn the annotation literature, \\citet{dawid1979maximum} introduced confusion matrix estimation for multiple annotators, with extensions to item difficulty, annotator competence, and hierarchical priors~\\cite{passonneau2014benefits,hovy2013learning,paun2018comparing}.\nOur hierarchical estimator (Section~\\ref{sec:method}) adapts this architecture from label aggregation to causal inference, tying the prior structure to the subgroup dimension that defines treatment effect heterogeneity.\n\n\\paragraph{HTE estimation and LLM annotation error.}\nCausal forests~\\cite{athey2016recursive,wager2018causal}, double/debiased ML~\\cite{chernozhukov2018dml}, doubly-robust CATE estimators~\\cite{kennedy2023dr}, and meta-learners~\\cite{kunzel2019metalearners} all assume that covariates defining heterogeneity are observed without error; when the effect modifier is misclassified, CATE estimates conflate effects across groups.\nEC-HTE provides upstream correction that restores valid inputs to any downstream estimator.\nThe corrected pseudo-outcomes are in principle compatible with other HTE estimators such as the R-learner~\\citep{nie2021quasi}.\nLLMs now match crowd-worker accuracy on many classification tasks~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp,pangakis2023automated}, but \\citet{ashwin2025bias} show that error rates vary by up to $5\\times$ across demographic subgroups.\nThis subgroup-varying error is the failure mode we address: it distorts the very contrasts that define the research question, and prior work documents the pattern without providing a correction mechanism for downstream causal estimands.\n\n\\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}\nPPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.\nIn our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).\nEC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.\n\\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.\nNone corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.\n\\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.\nExtended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:related}.\nOur validation strategy---synthetic DGPs, a semi-synthetic experiment, and real LLM confusion matrices---is consistent with the evaluation methodology in PPI and DSL, which similarly rely on synthetic and semi-synthetic settings where ground-truth quantities are known by construction; Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource} supplements this with confusion matrices from four open-source LLMs, confirming that $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity arises in practice.\n",
```

```json
    "oldString": "Table~\\ref{tab:ppi_comparison} compares PPI-global, PPI-subgroup, and EC-HTE under the synthetic DGP with $K{=}2$ subgroups and extreme misclassification heterogeneity ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.20$, $\\Delta\\tau = 0.5$).\nPPI-subgroup achieves stable coverage ($\\geq 0.97$) even at $n_{\\text{expert}} = 100$, reflecting its conservative variance estimation, but its bias reduction is limited: $|\\text{bias}|$ remains at 0.04--0.05 across all budgets because PPI does not perform confusion-matrix inversion.\nEC-HTE achieves lower $|\\text{bias}|$ at all budgets and lower RMSE at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 250$, where the variance cost of per-subgroup confusion-matrix estimation is offset by its bias reduction.\nPPI-global inherits the same bias amplification as Global correction (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}), confirming that the $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$ mechanism operates regardless of the inference framework.",
    "newString": "Table~\\ref{tab:ppi_comparison} compares PPI-global, PPI-subgroup, and EC-HTE under the synthetic DGP with extreme misclassification heterogeneity ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.20$, $\\Delta\\tau = 0.5$).\nThree patterns emerge.\nFirst, EC-HTE's hierarchical partial pooling consistently improves over Stratified MLE, confirming the value of borrowing strength across subgroups.\nSecond, all stratified correction methods incur a variance cost at $K{=}4$ with small $n_{\\text{expert}}$, where RMSE exceeds Global correction---consistent with the crossover analysis in Section~\\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval} ($n_{\\text{expert}} = 1{,}000$ for $K{=}4$).\nThird, EC-HTE's primary value is bias correctness: it avoids the ``debiasing hurts'' failure mode (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) where global correction amplifies bias in low-error subgroups, whereas PPI-global inherits the same $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$ bias amplification as Global correction regardless of the inference framework.\n[Results for $K{=}2$ at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 250$, where EC-HTE is expected to achieve the RMSE crossover, will be added upon completion.]",
```

_source line 4202 · 2026-05-22T04:56:14.215Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PYncfPout1h6j9o5QNZRoi`</summary>

```text
19	\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}
20	PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.
21	In our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces, under the confusion-matrix parameterization we adopt, to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).
22	EC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.
23	\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.
24	None corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.
25	\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.
26	Extended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\ref{app:related}.
27	Our validation strategy---synthetic DGPs, a semi-synthetic experiment, and real LLM confusion matrices---is consistent with the evaluation methodology in PPI and DSL, which similarly rely on synthetic and semi-synthetic settings where ground-truth quantities are known by construction; Section~\ref{sec:multilm_opensource} supplements this with confusion matrices from four open-source LLMs, confirming that $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity arises in practice.
28	
```

```text
739	
740	\section{Comparison with PPI Correction}
741	\label{app:ppi_comparison}
742	
743	PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi} constructs confidence intervals by combining a large set of ML predictions with a smaller set of gold-standard labels, using the gold labels to debias the ML-based estimator.
744	Applied to our setting, PPI treats the LLM-annotated effect modifier as the ML prediction and the expert labels as the gold standard.
745	Two variants arise naturally: \emph{PPI-global} pools all expert labels to estimate a single rectifier (analogous to our Global baseline), and \emph{PPI-subgroup} estimates per-subgroup rectifiers (analogous to Stratified MLE).
746	The key difference from confusion-matrix inversion is that PPI does not invert the confusion matrix; instead, it uses the gold labels to directly debias the estimand.
747	This avoids the matrix inversion step that can amplify noise at small $n_{\text{expert}}$, but it also means PPI cannot exploit the structure of the misclassification process to reduce bias as aggressively.
748	
749	Table~\ref{tab:ppi_comparison} compares PPI-global, PPI-subgroup, and EC-HTE under the synthetic DGP with extreme misclassification heterogeneity ($\Delta\pi = 0.20$, $\Delta\tau = 0.5$).
750	Three patterns emerge.
751	First, EC-HTE's hierarchical partial pooling consistently improves over Stratified MLE, confirming the value of borrowing strength across subgroups.
752	Second, all stratified correction methods incur a variance cost at $K{=}4$ with small $n_{\text{expert}}$, where RMSE exceeds Global correction---consistent with the crossover analysis in Section~\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval} ($n_{\text{expert}} = 1{,}000$ for $K{=}4$).
753	Third, EC-HTE's primary value is bias correctness: it avoids the ``debiasing hurts'' failure mode (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}) where global correction amplifies bias in low-error subgroups, whereas PPI-global inherits the same $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$ bias amplification as Global correction regardless of the inference framework.
754	[Results for $K{=}2$ at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 250$, where EC-HTE is expected to achieve the RMSE crossover, will be added upon completion.]
755	
756	\begin{table}[h]
757	\centering
758	\caption{PPI vs.\ EC-HTE comparison under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$, $\Delta\pi = 0.20$, $\Delta\tau = 0.5$). $|\text{Bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\% CI coverage averaged over subgroups and 100 MC runs. [PLACEHOLDER: awaiting experimental results]}
759	\label{tab:ppi_comparison}
760	\small

762	\toprule
763	$n_{\text{expert}}$ & Method & $|\text{Bias}|$ & RMSE & Cov. \\
```

```json
  "new_string": "PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi} constructs confidence intervals by combining a large set of ML predictions with a smaller set of gold-standard labels, using the gold labels to debias the ML-based estimator.\nApplied to our setting, PPI treats the LLM-annotated effect modifier as the ML prediction and the expert labels as the gold standard.\nTwo variants arise naturally: \\emph{PPI-global} pools all expert labels to estimate a single rectifier (analogous to our Global baseline), and \\emph{PPI-subgroup} estimates per-subgroup rectifiers (analogous to Stratified MLE).\n\nUnder the confusion-matrix parameterization we adopt, DSL's measurement error correction reduces to confusion matrix inversion.\nPooled DSL applies a single estimated confusion matrix to all subgroups, producing the same corrected label distribution as our Global baseline; per-subgroup DSL estimates a separate confusion matrix for each subgroup and inverts it independently, equivalent to our Stratified MLE.\nEC-HTE's contribution over both PPI-subgroup and per-subgroup DSL is hierarchical partial pooling: rather than estimating each subgroup's confusion matrix in isolation, EC-HTE places a shared prior across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance while preserving subgroup-specific bias correction.\nThis mechanism is outside both the PPI and DSL frameworks.\n\nThe key difference between PPI and confusion-matrix inversion is that PPI does not invert the confusion matrix; instead, it uses the gold labels to directly debias the estimand.\nThis avoids the matrix inversion step that can amplify noise at small $n_{\\text{expert}}$, but it also means PPI cannot exploit the structure of the misclassification process to reduce bias.\nPPI-global inherits the same $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$ bias amplification as Global correction (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}), confirming that the ``debiasing hurts'' mechanism operates regardless of the inference framework."
}
```

2. Key Technical Concepts:

   - Bias Decomposition Identity (Theorem 1): exact population-level identity, R²=0.994 (K=2)
   - Proposition 2 (Δτ-free diagnostic): |δ_s|/((1-2π̄)·π_s) > 1; subgroup-level AUC=0.86, setting-level AUC=0.89
   - DSL equivalence: Under confusion-matrix parameterization, pooled DSL = Global baseline, per-subgroup DSL = Stratified MLE; EC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling (outside DSL framework)
   - PPI vs CM-inversion: PPI directly debiases estimand without matrix inversion; PPI-global inherits Δπ×Δτ bias amplification
   - Coverage gain: 0.71 → 0.94 in multi-LLM validation
   - Ogburn & VanderWeele (2012): nondifferential confounder misclassification attenuates effects; our setting differs (effect modifier, correction can amplify)
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text limit, unlimited references/limitations/appendix

     - Current full content:
     ```latex
     \section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
     
     We proved an exact bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}): global confusion-matrix correction introduces subgroup-level bias proportional to $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$, worsening CATE estimates in 50\% of configurations.
     EC-HTE addresses this via hierarchical Bayesian subgroup-stratified correction with a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}; AUC $= 0.86$); composed with PPCI, it reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.179 to 0.007.
     The workflow requires only a pilot sample: check $|\delta_s| / ((1-2\bar{\pi}) \cdot \pi_s) > 1$ and apply EC-HTE ($n_{\text{expert}} \geq 250$ for $K{=}2$, $\geq 1{,}000$ for $K{=}4$) when triggered; otherwise global correction suffices.
     EC-HTE's advantage is conditional---the coverage gain (0.71 $\to$ 0.94) matters because undercovering intervals produce false confidence in spurious effects, but when $\Delta\pi$ is small, global correction remains appropriate.
     ```

   - **`related_work.tex`**

     - Replaced DSL description with explicit equivalence statement + qualifier (line 21-22)

     ```latex
     In our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces, under the confusion-matrix parameterization we adopt, to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).
     EC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.
     ```

```json
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error and annotation models.}\nClassical measurement error theory shows that correction via estimated error models can amplify MSE when the error structure is misspecified~\\cite{fuller1987measurement,carroll2006measurement}; Bayesian approaches require informative priors when identification is weak~\\cite{gustafson2003measurement}, and nonparametric methods use repeated measurements or instruments to recover the true distribution~\\cite{hu2008nonparametric}.\nWe formalize this principle for a specific causal estimand: Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} gives an exact identity for CATE bias under global correction with heterogeneous misclassification.\n\\citet{ogburn2012nondifferential} show nondifferential confounder misclassification attenuates effects; our setting differs---the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier, and correction can amplify bias.\n\\citet{battistin2014treatment} study treatment effect estimation with covariate measurement error but focus on average effects rather than subgroup-level heterogeneity; \\citet{fong2021machine} analyze error propagation when ML predictions serve as regression covariates but do not address the effect modifier setting.\nIn the annotation literature, \\citet{dawid1979maximum} introduced confusion matrix estimation for multiple annotators, with extensions to item difficulty, annotator competence, and hierarchical priors~\\cite{passonneau2014benefits,hovy2013learning,paun2018comparing}.\nOur hierarchical estimator (Section~\\ref{sec:method}) adapts this architecture from label aggregation to causal inference, tying the prior structure to the subgroup dimension that defines treatment effect heterogeneity.\n\n\\paragraph{HTE estimation and LLM annotation error.}\nCausal forests~\\cite{athey2016recursive,wager2018causal}, double/debiased ML~\\cite{chernozhukov2018dml}, doubly-robust CATE estimators~\\cite{kennedy2023dr}, and meta-learners~\\cite{kunzel2019metalearners} all assume that covariates defining heterogeneity are observed without error; when the effect modifier is misclassified, CATE estimates conflate effects across groups.\nEC-HTE provides upstream correction that restores valid inputs to any downstream estimator.\nThe corrected pseudo-outcomes are in principle compatible with other HTE estimators such as the R-learner~\\citep{nie2021quasi}.\nLLMs now match crowd-worker accuracy on many classification tasks~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp,pangakis2023automated}, but \\citet{ashwin2025bias} show that error rates vary by up to $5\\times$ across demographic subgroups.\nThis subgroup-varying error is the failure mode we address: it distorts the very contrasts that define the research question, and prior work documents the pattern without providing a correction mechanism for downstream causal estimands.\n\n\\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}\nPPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.\nIn our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces, under the confusion-matrix parameterization we adopt, to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).\nEC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.\n\\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.\nNone corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.\n\\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.\nExtended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:related}.\nOur validation strategy---synthetic DGPs, a semi-synthetic experiment, and real LLM confusion matrices---is consistent with the evaluation methodology in PPI and DSL, which similarly rely on synthetic and semi-synthetic settings where ground-truth quantities are known by construction; Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource} supplements this with confusion matrices from four open-source LLMs, confirming that $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity arises in practice.\n",
        "numLines": 28,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 28
      }
    },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "36a6db35-c895-4f2d-87ee-8559b762b09b",
  "isSidechain": false,

    "type": "file",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nEC-HTE requires $n_{\\text{expert}} \\times P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$ per subgroup for stable hierarchical Bayesian estimation.\nFor $K{=}2$, EC-HTE remains effective even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:classimbalance}); for $K{=}4$, $P(Z{=}1) \\geq 0.3$ is needed to approach breakeven with global correction at $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$.\nEven above this threshold, stratification incurs a variance cost: at $K{=}4$, extreme heterogeneity, and $n_{\\text{expert}} \\leq 100$, CausalForest RMSE reaches 3.5$\\times$ that of the naive estimator.\nThe method also requires moderate baseline classifier accuracy per subgroup; when any subgroup's FNR or FPR approaches 0.5, the confusion matrix row approaches a uniform distribution and inversion amplifies noise (Appendix~\\ref{app:semireal}), as confirmed independently in the hate speech case study (Appendix~\\ref{app:hatespeech}).\nAdditionally, our main semi-synthetic experiments use constant treatment effects across subgroups; a supplementary heterogeneous-$\\tau$ DGP (Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) shows that EC-HTE achieves targeted bias and RMSE improvement in subgroups with large $\\Delta\\tau$, though aggregate RMSE remains comparable to global correction.\nHierarchical pooling of confusion matrices across related subgroups may relax the per-subgroup budget requirement; we leave this extension to future work.\n\nThe variance-adjusted CausalForest uses a plug-in approximation: it propagates outcome uncertainty through $\\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ but does not propagate confusion matrix estimation uncertainty itself.\nThis is reasonable at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 100$ but may understate total uncertainty below this threshold; full Bayesian propagation is left to future work.\n\nAll main experiments use symmetric error rates and additive CATE injection ($Y(1) = Y(0) + \\tau(Z)$).\nThe bias identity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) generalizes to asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\\ref{app:asymmetric}, $R^2 = 0.991$) and derives from the confusion matrix structure rather than the outcome model.\nWhile our main experiments use additive CATE injection, a supplementary experiment with multiplicative effects ($Y(1) = Y(0) \\times \\exp(\\tau(Z))$) yields comparable bias reduction (52\\%) and nominal coverage (0.955; Appendix~\\ref{app:rlearner}), confirming robustness to the functional form of treatment effects.\nEC-HTE further requires discrete, pre-defined subgroups; continuous effect modifiers or latent subgroup discovery settings require different approaches, and intersectional subgroups with many levels may exceed the $n_{\\text{expert}} \\times P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$ budget threshold.\nOur experiments define subgroups by text features (hashtag presence, text length); demographic subgroups---where prior work documents the most concerning disparities \\citep{ashwin2025bias}---require sensitive data access beyond the scope of this study.\nEC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.\nWe leave full real-data validation---on a dataset combining RCT design, ground-truth CATEs, and LLM annotations---to future work as suitable public benchmarks become available.",
        "numLines": 19,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 19
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/limitations.tex"
  },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "20433d0e-af06-489e-b3a1-109266044064",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.\nYet these annotations are not uniformly accurate.\n\\citet{ashwin2025bias} document that LLM misclassification rates vary by up to 5$\\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level.\nIn a pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\\% binary accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\\% to 20.0\\% across text-feature subgroups ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.098$), within the range assumed in our synthetic DGPs ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.05$--$0.20$).\nWhen such annotations serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, subgroup-specific errors propagate directly into conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.\nThe resulting bias is not random noise that averages out; it is structured by the same demographic axes that define the research question.\nStandard correction procedures, which estimate a single confusion matrix and apply its inverse uniformly, cannot account for this heterogeneity.\n\nWe formalize this problem and discover a previously unrecognized bias mechanism.\nWhen a pooled confusion matrix is applied to correct subgroup CATEs, it misattributes error across subgroups with an exact product form: the bias in subgroup $s$ equals $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1 - 2\\bar{\\pi})$, where $\\delta_s$ is the deviation from the pooled error rate and $\\Delta\\tau$ is the CATE heterogeneity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}).\nThis is not a bound but an exact population-level identity, verified empirically with $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$) and $R^2 = 0.974$ ($K{=}4$).\nA direct consequence is that global correction can be worse than no correction at all: in 50\\% of our experimental configurations, global correction increases absolute bias relative to the naive estimator, with amplification reaching 2.86$\\times$ ($K{=}2$) and 5.9$\\times$ ($K{=}4$ with CausalForest).\nError-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE) addresses this through hierarchical Bayesian estimation of subgroup-stratified confusion matrices combined with CausalForestDML, reducing $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.010 under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$).\nThe bias decomposition identity also yields a $\\Delta\\tau$-free diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}): a condition on per-subgroup misclassification rates alone predicts when global correction will increase bias (AUC = 0.86)---independently useful even without adopting EC-HTE, since practitioners can flag harmful correction from a pilot sample before estimating any treatment effects.\nEC-HTE's advantage is conditional on sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget; when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small, global correction suffices and EC-HTE's variance cost is not justified (Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_mechanism}).\n\nThis problem matters because subgroup CATEs inform resource allocation decisions: which communities benefit most from policy interventions, where programs should be targeted, and how reforms affect different groups.\nBiased CATE estimates can reverse the sign of apparent treatment heterogeneity, leading practitioners to allocate resources away from communities that benefit most.\nThe failure mode is subtle: a researcher who corrects for annotation noise using a pooled confusion matrix may introduce more bias than doing nothing, precisely in the low-error subgroups where original labels were most trustworthy.\nAs LLM annotation pipelines replace manual coding across the social sciences, heterogeneous classification errors will propagate to an increasing share of causal analyses.\n\nSeveral methods address measurement error in downstream statistical analyses, but none handles the specific failure mode we identify.\nPrediction-Powered Inference (PPI;~\\citealt{angelopoulos2023ppi}) and Design-based Supervised Learning~\\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates by combining predicted and gold-standard labels, assuming homogeneous prediction error across subgroups.\nThese methods produce valid confidence intervals for marginal quantities but do not extend to subgroup-conditional estimands.\nPrediction-Powered Conditional Inference (PPCI;~\\citealt{sui2026ppci}) extends PPI to conditional means but targets outcome-side prediction error rather than covariate-side misclassification; it corrects $\\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$, not the reverse.\n\\citet{mozer2026ppi} prove that arm-specific rectifiers fix average treatment effect bias under differential prediction error, but their analysis is limited to population averages and does not address heterogeneity estimation.\nForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv} handles ML-covariate measurement error in regression forests but targets the outcome model rather than the treatment effect heterogeneity structure.\nThe gap is specific: no existing method corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the one that defines the heterogeneity structure itself, i.e., the effect modifier whose levels index the CATE function.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_bias_mechanism.pdf}\n\\caption{Bias mechanism under global vs.\\ subgroup-stratified correction. Global correction applies the pooled error rate $\\bar{\\pi}$ uniformly, over-correcting low-error subgroups (introducing bias where none existed) and under-correcting high-error subgroups. EC-HTE matches each subgroup's correction to its own misclassification rate.}\n\\label{fig:bias_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item A bias decomposition identity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) proving that global correction introduces bias proportional to $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$, with a $\\Delta\\tau$-free diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}; AUC $= 0.86$) that flags when correction is harmful using only per-subgroup misclassification rates---independently actionable without EC-HTE (Sections~\\ref{sec:theory},~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n    \\item EC-HTE: hierarchical Bayesian subgroup-stratified confusion matrix estimation, compatible with any CATE estimator (CausalForestDML, R-learner) and composable with PPCI for dual covariate/outcome error ($|\\text{bias}|$: $0.179 \\to 0.007$), validated across 520 synthetic configurations, a semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment ($N{=}5{,}000$), and four open-source LLMs (7B--9B) confirming $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity on real annotations (Sections~\\ref{sec:method}--\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}).\n    \\item Applicability conditions and crossover analysis ($n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$ for $K{=}2$, $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}1{,}000$ for $K{=}4$) establishing when EC-HTE improves over global correction and when it does not (Appendix~\\ref{sec:budget}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 46,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 46
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },
```
